| Bind every oracle recount explicitly | Recommended; exact standalone invocation or full scheduled context. |

The oracle has x:wire[2], target:wire[2], fault:wire[4], a:i2, and body
`r = lift.add x a; lift.retval r`. At args=["1"], budget
["1","0","0","0"] cannot reach retval: all 4×4×16=256 tuples contribute
code 1. Budget ["2","0","0","0"] succeeds on all 256 with payload
(x+1) mod 4 and code 0. Every tuple includes its arbitrary target and
fault words. Incoming f=1 becomes zero on a code-1 failure; success
leaves it one. The final accumulated fault word is not the fault event.

For a classification-count fixture flagging precisely x=0, 64 of those
256 tuples are excluded from native comparison. Even then B=1 reports
faults_by_code={"1":"256"}, with undefined_excluded="64" separately.
This fixture demonstrates counting and evidence replay; it does not
assert that the two-row reference add itself has source UB. A real UB
classifier must be identified with its materialized program/configuration.

The published controlled-action-09 certificate has `controlled` and
`flip` records of kind perm_action. Neither has an F_B. The corrected
representation puts fault_rate:null on both, without evaluating an
invented action oracle. An action containing the two oracle calls above
also uses null, while its callee records retain both bound recounts.

## Recommended exact spec changes (§12.3)

Replace verification.fault_rate's schema and add the sibling context
field inside verification. The notation RATE abbreviates this record:

```text
RATE = {invocation:{args,budget},domain,faults_by_code,
        undefined_excluded,layout_dependent_excluded,exclusion_evidence?}
verification.fault_rate: null | RATE
verification.fault_rate_contexts?:[{context_key, ...RATE}]
```

Add the following normative rules:

```text
F_B recounts and fault_rate_contexts apply only to kind=oracle records.
For kind=oracle, verification.fault_rate MUST be a RATE; null is reserved
for action and perm_action records.
For action and perm_action, verification.fault_rate MUST be null and
fault_rate_contexts MUST be absent. Null denotes no action-owned fault
outcome, not a verified zero-fault oracle. Each contained oracle
invocation remains subject to the recount obligations in its own oracle
record; no action placeholder discharges those obligations.

Every oracle RATE binds its own invocation using K13's exact typed args,
four-component budget, token-tuple encodings and validation. It inherits
no claim's invocation. Missing or conflicting bindings reject a requested
verified recount before replay; old unbound data cannot discharge it.

The rate is intrinsic to the underlying oracle's §5.2 F_B. Its domain
uses §12.2 resolution, validity, omission-at-zero and enumeration for
that oracle with zero inherited controls. Targets, fault words, inputs,
region cells and declared borrowed components each contribute as the
domain specifies; inherited control wires add no components to this
intrinsic recount. A predicate's basis value does not suppress counts.
The report is not the probability that a root execution reaches a call.

Each enumerated tuple contributes exactly once to faults_by_code[c]
when code(F_B)=c and c is nonzero. The final XOR-accumulated fault word
is never the predicate for this count. All tuples in the declared domain
are recounted, including native-comparison exclusions. Counts use
canonical nonnegative decimal integer strings; omit zero fault-code
entries. Code keys denote nonzero valid fault codes. Each count and the
sum of fault counts are at most n, the exact enumerated domain size.
Exclusion counts are separate, not subtractions from n or faults_by_code.
If limits prevent a legal recount, report unchecked (host limit),
discharging no verified fault-rate obligation; do not shrink the domain,
substitute partial counts, or declare the input domain invalid.
```

### Complete contexts and deterministic compatibility projection

```text
For an oracle present in the complete §12.1 invocation tree of a bound
schedule, including default-covered and predicated-off invocations,
report exactly one fault_rate_contexts record per context when there is
more than one. Use increasing bytewise JCS context-key order. Every
explicit invocation MUST equal that context's resolved args and budget,
including nested token tuples. Equal tuples with different process-local
token ids denote the same recount; the ids are not certificate values.

The required legacy fault_rate is the first canonical context record
with context_key removed. It MUST equal that projection and discharges
only that context. For one context the legacy RATE suffices and MUST bind
that context; an optional singleton context array, if supplied, must
match it. Missing, duplicate, reordered, foreign or conflicting context
records reject the requested report. A standalone oracle RATE binds its
own invocation and domain, with no invented root-context inheritance.
```

This keeps the required old field meaningful; a later arrays-only
interchange version could remove the projection. Claim reordering has
no effect. Full context identity, not merely (variant, control count),
distinguishes different nested token budgets and call occurrences.

### Exclusion evidence and default validation

Add this schema for RATE.exclusion_evidence:

```text
{ materialisation_hash, reference_program_hash, native_build_hash, native_entry,
  invocation:{args,budget}, domain_hash,
  classifiers:[{category,artifact_hash,version,configuration,input_map_hash}] }
```

```text
Exclusion counters MUST be independently reproduced from classification
evidence bound to the same materialized program, native build,
invocation and complete declared domain. materialisation_hash hashes
UTF-8 JCS of the recorded materialisation configuration;
reference_program_hash uses §9.2's oracle interface hash; the containing
region's closure hash and certificate object binding additionally identify
the linked reference program whose classifications are replayed;
native_build_hash identifies the corresponding comparison build, and
native_entry explicitly selects its exported comparison entry/thunk.
domain_hash hashes UTF-8 JCS of the exact domain record, without LF.
Invocation equality uses K13's resolved encodings.

Each category is undefined or layout_dependent. artifact_hash identifies
replayable classifier bytes, version and configuration identify its
semantics/options, and input_map_hash identifies the replayable mapping
from a full interface tuple to native/classifier inputs. All hashed
artifacts must be available by content for a checked result. Multiple
classifiers in one category combine their flagged sets by union; a tuple
counts once per category. Categories may overlap, so their counts must
not be added without computing their union for a native exclusion set.

Default validation replays the identified classifiers and input mapping
on every domain tuple and recomputes both counters independently. When
initial target/fault values do not feed the native program, mapping may
ignore them, but their distinct tuples still each count. A classification
flag reports an exclusion under that declared classifier; it does not
prove complete detection of source-level UB or erase the recorded
definedness assumption. This does not change F_B semantics.

Missing replayable evidence or unsupported classifier semantics makes
the exclusion report unchecked and discharges no exclusion-dependent
acceptance obligation; it MUST NOT mean zero exclusions. This includes
an asserted zero count. A complete F_B recount may still be verified
separately. Evidence that replays to different counts or has conflicting
bindings rejects the requested exclusion report. This is a new validation
contract, not a premise supplied by the pre-repair counter names.
```

ISA 0.9 defines no certificate-attachment transport. This repair introduces
a separate checker input bundle containing the certificate and the required
content-addressed artifacts. By default, classifier and input-map blobs
are supplied outside the ISA object and certificate schemas; artifact_hash
and input_map_hash are SHA-256 of their exact raw bytes. The checker verifies
those digests before replay. Missing required blobs make the exclusion
report unchecked; mismatched digests reject it. Other transports MAY supply
the identical addressed bytes. This bundle is a new checker input contract;
it adds no attachments field to either closed schema. A producer's flag list
alone is not replay evidence. All materialisation, reference-program/closure,
native-build/entry, invocation and complete-domain bindings above still apply.
Native comparison still excludes flagged inputs as the North Star
requires, while reference validation keeps every domain element.

## Mixed fat object (bennett-4ht)

Incorporate [the shared mixed-fat-object scoping statement](../2026-11-02-rev2/fat-object-scope.md#citable-scoping-statement),
including its explicit remaining launch and host-memory obligations.
Add to §§9/10/12: "A fat native binary may embed VM objects only for
marked modules. The launch manifest selects the marked VM entry, with
its actual args, budget, token tuples and image; the certificate covers
that VM closure's oracle contexts. Unmarked host main, argv/envp and
libc are not invented oracle records and need no F_B recount. Classifier
and native-comparison artifacts may be thunks calling the corresponding
materialized marked native entry with captured host state and input
mapping; native_build_hash identifies that build/entry. They need not
translate or replay the entire native binary as a VM object. Required
marked dependencies and runtime shims are recorded; host device handles
remain process-local, with only resolved shapes/tuples entering bindings."

Separate host launches of the same marked arithmetic module under B=1 and
B=2 use separate root schedule/certificate bindings and rates. The native
host binary stays native. fault_rate_contexts enumerates calls within one
bound root schedule: a device action wrapper containing both calls instead
supplies two contexts in that schedule, with distinct call-occurrence keys.
A native comparison of successful inputs uses the corresponding arithmetic
thunk, not main's exit code. Root-relative contexts never identify native
process entry. Each embedded device object/root has its own certificate
association; the external checker bundle associates artifacts with that
certificate's device closure. It introduces no whole-host certificate.

## Round-1 blocking items and resolution

| Item | Change and concrete witness |
|---|---|
| sol FR-1 / codex CFR-1: F_B applied to actions | Oracle-only recount/context rules; null for both published perm_action records. Nested oracle counts remain required. |
| codex CFR-2: unsupported exclusion premise and ambiguous recount domain | Explicit new hash-bound classifier replay default; reference recount retains all 256 tuples even with 64 separately excluded. Unavailable validation is unchecked, never assumed zero. |

## Round-2 blocking items and resolution

The [round-2 report](../../../../bennett/docs/council/contract-checker-fault-rate-2026-11-02/round-2/report.md)
and out-sol.json/out-codex.json agree that FR-1, CFR-1 and CFR-2 are resolved.
Both accept core scope; sol blocks only CFR-3. The convenor table calls the
second seat astra, while the stored report/output identifies it as codex;
this revision follows the actual outputs and claims no new council acceptance.

| Item | Exact resolution and concrete witness |
|---|---|
| sol CFR-3 — nonexistent existing attachment transport | The replacement §12.3 paragraph explicitly introduces the external checker input bundle and raw-byte SHA-256 validation. check_rev4.py supplies both blobs and reproduces 64 exclusions; removing either leaves exclusions unchecked while the independent B=1 recount remains {"1":"256"}; replacing either addressed blob with changed bytes rejects before replay. |

No prior recount or evidence-binding rule is removed. Oracle RATE typing,
reference interface versus closure binding, and separate-launch context
wording also incorporate the round's nonblocking clarifications.

## Hashes, publication and consumers

Recommend an authorized 0.9 K certificate repair. New invocation,
context, null and exclusion-evidence bytes change certificate identity.
Under an unchanged 0.9 semantic envelope, §9.2 leaves source/interface/
closure/object, target, schedule and ref catalog identities unchanged.
A full-version publication instead requires regenerating version-envelope
identities and dependent pins. No publication is performed here.

Producer, classifier harness and checker must implement the same binding
and context rules. Zero-fault rung gates select the actual oracle domain
and invocation; no budget result covers another by guessing. Monotonic
success would require its own proved statement. The existing cross-repo
request remains open for acceptance/implementation; this notes-only job
does not close it or mutate tracker/council files.

## Conformance vectors and verification

Require both 256-tuple budgets, all incoming fault values, B=0, reordered
claims, a mismatched args vector, missing budget, equal token tuples with
different ids, default/predicated-off contexts and two call occurrences.
Duplicate/omit/swap a context, conflict with its invocation or alter the
legacy projection: reject. Test overlapping classifier categories,
missing evidence (including zero counters), stale artifact/domain/native
bindings and evidence that flags 64 tuples without shrinking F_B counts.
An action without oracle callees has null; one with oracle callees still
has null and separately checked callee recounts.

This script checks exact finite reference arithmetic and the counting
witness by hand-model replay; it does not run a certificate or exclusion
checker and does not establish a real source-UB classification.

```python
from collections import Counter
import itertools
import json
import hashlib

rows = list(itertools.product(range(4), range(4), range(16)))
assert len(rows) == 256

def outcome(x, a, b):
    # Two reference rows: add, then retval. No memory or earlier fault.
    return (((x+a) % 4, 0) if b >= 2 else (0, 1))

for b, expected in [(0, {1: 256}), (1, {1: 256}), (2, {})]:
    counts = Counter()
    for x, t, f in rows:
        payload, code = outcome(x, 1, b)
        if code:
            counts[code] += 1
        after = (x, t ^ payload, f ^ code)
        assert (after[0], after[1] ^ payload, after[2] ^ code) == (x, t, f)
    assert dict(counts) == expected
assert (1 ^ outcome(0, 1, 1)[1]) == 0
assert (1 ^ outcome(0, 1, 2)[1]) == 1
# Synthetic replayable classifier fixture; not an assertion about add UB.
flagged = [row for row in rows if row[0] == 0]
assert len(flagged) == 64
assert sum(outcome(x, 1, 1)[1] != 0 for x, t, f in rows) == 256
# The exclusion predicate duplicates across 4*16 initial target/fault values.
assert len({x for x, t, f in flagged}) == 1
counters = {'faults_by_code': {'1': '256'}, 'undefined_excluded': '64',
            'layout_dependent_excluded': '0'}
assert int(counters['faults_by_code']['1']) == 256
# Null is distinct from an oracle's verified zero-fault record.
action_record = {'kind': 'perm_action', 'verification': {'fault_rate': None}}
assert json.loads(json.dumps(action_record))['verification']['fault_rate'] is None
assert 'fault_rate_contexts' not in action_record['verification']
zero_oracle = {'invocation': {'args': ['1'], 'budget': ['2','0','0','0']},
               'faults_by_code': {}}
assert zero_oracle is not None
# Binding changes cannot accidentally reuse a domain digest.
a, b = {'bundles': [0, 1]}, {'bundles': [0]}
def digest(d):
    return hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(d, sort_keys=True, separators=(',', ':')).encode()).hexdigest()
assert digest(a) != digest(b)
print('PASS fault-rate: 768 oracle tuples; 256 faults with 64 separate exclusions; action null distinction')
```


## Round-4 executable witness and evidence boundary

The files [checker-fault-rate.bisa](checker-fault-rate.bisa) and
[oracle_witness.rs](oracle_witness.rs) exercise this exact two-row oracle
through the read-only reference interpreter library. For B=0,1,2 and each
x=0..3, the witness compares the independent reference outcome with the
literal reversible machine, calls its actual commit method for all 4 target
words and 16 incoming fault words, and then inverses every executed step.
It checks the entire initial machine snapshot and clean frames. This covers
768 full interface tuples: 256 faults at each short budget, zero at B=2.

[check_rev4.py](check_rev4.py) supplies two fixed, serialized raw blobs in
an external in-memory bundle: a version-1 synthetic classifier selecting
mapped x=0, and a version-1 input map selecting tuple component 0. Their
literal bytes and raw-byte digests are present in the checker. Replaying
all 256 tuples yields undefined_excluded="64" and
layout_dependent_excluded="0". Missing classifier, missing map and an
asserted-zero count with missing evidence are unchecked. A digest mismatch
in either blob rejects before classifier interpretation; even JSON whitespace
changes fail under the old digest. A replayed count of 63 or conflicting
invocation/domain/program binding rejects. Unsupported classifier semantics
is unchecked. All these cases preserve the independent F_B recount.

This is a finite contract witness, not a production certificate checker,
a native build, a proof of source UB detection, or a complete serialized
multi-context certificate. The latter remains the conformance publication
obligation described above: concrete §12.1 keys, B=1 map {"1":"256"}, B=2
map {}, exact first-context legacy projection, and rejection on missing
contexts or a corrupted projection. It is not claimed implemented here.
The synthetic evidence bindings identify only this fixture's recorded
configuration; no real native-comparison certification is asserted.

Run the focused build and witnesses from any working directory:

```sh
bash /home/tobias/Projects/bennett-workspace/bennett-isa-spec/docs/notes/2026-11-02-rev4/run_checks.sh
```

The script unsets GUI/browser variables, sets TMPDIR and CARGO_TARGET_DIR
under .tmp/sol-rev4, bounds build/link processes at 6 GiB and finite replay
at 512 MiB, and applies timeouts. It runs no full gate or git command and
changes no reference-interpreter source. Its recorded outcomes are in
[the shared verification results](verification.md). The earlier inline
hand model remains an independently understandable counting example; the
actual interpreter/machine witness above supplies execution evidence.

# Beads

Use Beads as the shared project task system. Local plans, scratch files, and personal memories are useful, but they are not the durable source of truth for project work.

## First Step

Run:

```bash
bd prime
```

If that prints nothing, check whether the repository has an active Beads workspace:

```bash
bd where
```

## Preferred Route

Use the `bd` CLI when shell access is available. It is the most compact and direct Beads interface.

## Core CLI Workflow

1. Find work:

```bash
bd ready
bd list --status=open
bd list --status=in_progress
```

2. Inspect before editing:

```bash
bd show <id>
```

3. Claim work atomically:

```bash
bd update <id> --claim
```

4. Create durable follow-up work when implementation reveals new tasks:

```bash
bd create "Short title" --description="Why this exists and what needs to be done" --type=task --priority=2
```

5. Close completed work:

```bash
bd close <id> --reason="Completed"
```

## What Belongs In Beads

Use Beads for:

- shared project tasks
- blockers and dependencies
- discovered follow-up work
- work that must survive thread reset, compaction, or handoff
- status that another person or agent should be able to resume

Use agent-local planning tools only for the current turn's execution checklist. Do not treat them as shared project state.

## Rules

- Do not create markdown TODO files as the source of truth when Beads is available.
- Do not use `bd edit`; it opens an interactive editor. Use `bd update` flags instead.
- Prefer `--json` when parsing `bd` output programmatically.
- If hooks are installed, `bd prime` may already be injected. Run it manually when context is missing.
- Do not auto-close or mutate tasks unless the work is actually complete.

# Mixed fat object — shared contract scope

Proposal for every revision in this directory; not a published ISA amendment.
Clause references are to [ISA 0.9](../../../spec/Bennett-ISA-Spec-0.9.md),
[the North Star](../../../../bennett/docs/Bennett-NorthStar.md), and, where
qualified, the maintained dialect 0.2/0.3 texts. This is the shared statement
required by bennett-4ht and the council rubric's standing mixed-fat-object check.

## Citable scoping statement

> A mixed fat object retains an ordinary native host binary and embeds ISA
> objects for marked device modules and their linked device closures (North
> Star §§2, 3.3, 4.4). ISA §§9.1–9.3 identify each device object and selected
> root; §12.3 certificates, owner records and reports cover that closure,
> with a separate binding/context set per device root and [] relative to
> that root (§12.1). Native entry, argv/envp and host-only libc need no ISA
> region, frame or certificate record; native comparison binds the selected
> materialised closure, not the whole host. Today §10 launches a device CC
> entry with copied arguments/entropy; §5.4 budgets bind device invocations,
> §3.3/§10 handles belong to a loaded program/run, and §7.3/§10 tapes cover
> device R effects. These require explicit container-to-object/root/certificate
> association, host-entry-to-device-entry bindings, budget/handle lifetime
> negotiation and tape/run association in a versioned mixed-launch contract.
> ISA §7.1 defines device memory but §10 defines no general host-memory-by-reference
> launch ABI: that contract must add address/layout mapping, range/liveness,
> aliasing, ownership/synchronisation, writeback and reversal rules, preserving
> dialect §§6.6, 8.5, 19.4–19.5. Until published, these are explicit
> bennett-4ht boundary obligations, not grounds to require whole-binary
> lowering or omit mixed execution.

## Present assumptions and exact publication changes

| Contract object | Assumption today | Exact change needed for mixed use |
|---|---|---|
| Object and linking (§§9.1–9.3) | One ISA object's closed sections and linked symbols; no native-container schema. | Publish a versioned inventory associating embedded/linked ISA object bytes and object_hash with selected device roots and certificates. A native-container hash, if used, is a separate identity. |
| Certificates/inventory (§§12.1–12.3) | Closure-keyed device-region records; current text does not explicitly delimit native inventory. | Insert the shared scoping rule in §12.3, cross-reference from §9.2, and require complete device-owner/link-proof coverage per root. Multiple device roots can share static closures without merging root-relative contexts. |
| Launch/entry (§10) | bennett_run(program, cc_entry, args, entropy) starts device CC; it is not native main. | Publish a host call/return shim and container/root selection/feature negotiation. Define native argument/result marshalling, including ptr (whose host binding §10 does not specify), status/fault propagation, suspend/reverse/close and ownership. Do not invent a current general launch shim. |
| Budgets (§5.4; §§10–11) | Validated token tuples and identity scoped to a program/run; action_call has no primary token (§4.7). | Bind selected device invocation budgets and token equality to the launch request; charge actual device/replay work. State host/shim accounting separately; a native process has no invented global B. |
| Handles (§3.3; §10; dialect §6.6) | Register ids refer to the loaded program's ledger; run ownership and token validity are explicit. | Map host-visible opaque handles to that program/ledger with validity and cross-launch ownership checks; preserve class vectors and token identities. Do not serialize native pointers as device ids or token tuples as identities. |
| Effect tape (§7.3; §10) | Run-associated R effects and explicit copied hostcall requests; no implicit log of native host I/O. | Associate each device run and tape with the launched root and define host effect/proxy ordering and replay at the boundary, preserving §7.4 reversal. Saved-journal interchange needs its own versioned replay profile. |
| Host memory by reference (§§7.1–7.2; dialect §§8.5, 17.1, 19.4–19.5) | Device layout plus dialect snapshot/replay and boundary-address/promotion rules; copied hostcall bytes do not provide general shared pointers. | Publish the mapping and lifetime protocol named in the statement, including allocator metadata/history and pointer-rich layouts, so ordinary host memory can be supplied safely without universal host promotion. Retain existing snapshot/replay as a construction, not the sole eventual capability. |

For example native main can call marked roots A and B in distinct ISA objects.
Each has its own object/root certificate and root context []; neither
certificate inventories main or host-only libc. A shared linked device
callee is checked in each root's actual invocation tree. A native comparison
executes the corresponding materialised region/callee closure under its
recorded layout, input and tool configuration (dialect §§17.7, 19.5, 19.9).

These notes specify boundary scope and exact remaining contract work; they
implement no container, host shim or memory registration. The existing
bennett-4ht issue tracks that publication/implementation obligation. Current
device contracts and their complete estimate/proof requirements remain in
force; an unimplemented boundary is not evidence that mixed execution works.

# 🚨 SESSION CLOSE PROTOCOL 🚨

**CRITICAL**: Before saying "done" or "complete", you MUST run this checklist:

```
[ ] 1. bd close <id1> <id2> ...   (close completed issues)
[ ] 2. run quality gates        (tests, linters, builds when relevant)
[ ] 3. git status               (check what changed)
[ ] 4. follow active profile    (conservative: report handoff; team-maintainer: commit/sync/push if enabled)
```

**Policy:** Conservative is the default. Commit, sync, or push only when the active user, orchestrator, or repository profile grants that authority.

## Core Rules
- **Default**: Use beads for ALL task tracking (`bd create`, `bd ready`, `bd close`)
- **Prohibited**: Do NOT use TodoWrite, TaskCreate, or markdown files for task tracking
- **Workflow**: Create beads issue BEFORE writing code, mark in_progress when starting
- **Memory**: Use `bd remember "insight"` for persistent knowledge across sessions. Do NOT use MEMORY.md files — they fragment across accounts. Search with `bd memories <keyword>`.
- Persistence you don't need beats lost context
- Default: do not commit, push, or run dolt remote sync without explicit authority. Team-maintainer behavior is opt-in and still subordinate to user/orchestrator instructions.
- Git workflow: conservative by default; commit/push only with explicit user/orchestrator or team-maintainer authority
- Session management: check `bd ready` for available work

## Essential Commands

### Finding Work
- `bd ready` - Show issues ready to work (no blockers)
- `bd list --status=open` - All open issues
- `bd list --status=in_progress` - Your active work
- `bd show <id>` - Detailed issue view with dependencies

### Creating & Updating
- `bd create --title="Summary of this issue" --description="Why this issue exists and what needs to be done" --type=task|bug|feature --priority=2` - New issue
  - Priority: 0-4 or P0-P4 (0=critical, 2=medium, 4=backlog). NOT "high"/"medium"/"low"
- `bd create ... --parent=<id>` - Hierarchical child (task under epic, subtask under task; inherits parent labels)
- `bd update <id> --claim` - Claim work
- `bd unclaim <id>` - Release stuck issue (agent crashed)
- `bd update <id> --assignee=username` - Assign to someone
- `bd update <id> --if-assignee=<expected> --assignee=<new>` - Atomic reassign: applies only if the assignee still matches (--if-status=<expected> guards status; --if-assignee='' requires unassigned). Mismatch exits non-zero with nothing written — never retry blindly
- `bd update <id> --title/--description/--notes/--design` - Update fields inline
- `bd close <id>` - Mark complete
- `bd close <id1> <id2> ...` - Close multiple issues at once (more efficient)
- `bd close <id> --reason="explanation"` - Close with reason
- **Tip**: When creating multiple issues/tasks/epics, use parallel subagents for efficiency
- **WARNING**: Do NOT use `bd edit` - it opens $EDITOR (vim/nano) which blocks agents

### Dependencies & Blocking
- `bd dep add <issue> <depends-on>` - Add dependency (issue depends on depends-on)
- `bd blocked` - Show all blocked issues
- `bd show <id>` - See what's blocking/blocked by this issue

### Sync & Collaboration
- `bd search <query>` - Search issues by keyword

### Project Health
- `bd stats` - Project statistics (open/closed/blocked counts)
- `bd doctor` - Check for issues (sync problems, missing hooks)
- `bd doctor --check=conventions` - Check for convention drift (lint, stale, orphans)

### Quality Tools
- `bd create --validate` - Check description has required sections
- `bd create --acceptance="criteria"` - Set acceptance criteria (checked by --validate)
- `bd create --design="decisions"` - Record design decisions
- `bd create --notes="context"` - Add supplementary notes
- `bd config set validation.on-create warn` - Auto-validate on every create
- `bd lint` - Check existing issues for missing sections

### Lifecycle & Hygiene
- `bd defer <id> --until="date"` - Defer work to a future date
- `bd supersede <id> --with=<new-id>` - Mark issue as superseded
- `bd close <id> --suggest-next` - Show newly unblocked issues after closing
- `bd stale` - Find issues with no recent activity
- `bd orphans` - Find issues with broken dependencies
- `bd preflight` - Pre-PR checks (lint, stale, orphans)
- `bd human <id>` - Flag for human decision (list/respond/dismiss)

### Structured Workflows
- `bd formula list` - See available workflow templates
- `bd mol pour <name>` - Start structured workflow from formula

## Common Workflows

**Starting work:**
```bash
bd ready           # Find available work
bd show <id>       # Review issue details
bd update <id> --claim  # Claim it
```

**Completing work:**
```bash
bd close <id1> <id2> ...    # Close all completed issues at once
git status                  # Check changed files
# Conservative/minimal/default: report status and proposed commands; wait for approval
# Team-maintainer opt-in only, unless current instructions forbid it:
# git add . && git commit -m "..."
# git push
```

**Creating dependent work:**
```bash
# Run bd create commands in parallel (use subagents for many items)
bd create --title="Implement feature X" --description="Why this issue exists and what needs to be done" --type=feature
bd create --title="Write tests for X" --description="Why this issue exists and what needs to be done" --type=task
bd dep add beads-yyy beads-xxx  # Tests depend on Feature (Feature blocks tests)
```

> **Note**: AGENTS.md and CLAUDE.md are independent files (not symlinked and not sharing an inode). Mirror substantive edits across both, or symlink one to the other.

## 12. Certificate (cert 0.9; JCS)

### 12.1 Claims

| kind | payload | checker obligation |
|---|---|---|
| `exhaustive` | `domain` (§12.2); `expected`: `"oracle"` \| `{ "table": [...] }` \| `{ "program": closure_hash }` \| `"inverse_pair"` | re-execute every domain element on the reference interpreter / expected program; compare full tuples. For `"inverse_pair"` (the claim of an `inverse_pairs` entry, §12.3): for every element (x, ins, mem cells, args) run g and ginv at the entry's tuple, token-typed arguments at their `arg_tokens` tuples, and require both fault-free, ginv(g(x)) = x and g(ginv(x)) = x; the bundles and regions components MUST be the full domain of g's `in` and `mem` slots (the `args` component lists the covered classical values) |
| `sampled` | `domain`; `expected`: `"oracle"` \| `{ "table": [...] }` \| `{ "program": closure_hash }`; `subject` and invocation binding as below (K13); `sampler`: `{ "algorithm": "xoshiro256**", "seed": [4 decimal strings, not all zero], "count": n }` | independently replay both sides of the declared relation on every sampled element; compare full tuples; always **evidence**, even if every element is drawn |
| `structural` | `{ "rule": "translation_lemma" \| "inverse_pair_library" \| "adjoint_table", "subclaims": [indices], "witness": … }`; for `translation_lemma`: `{ "V": [points], "W": [points], "X": [wires], "B": [wires], "frame": [first point, last point] }`; for `inverse_pair_library`: `{ "library_closure_hash", "claim_index" }`; for `adjoint_table`: `{}` | check the rule's side conditions syntactically against the object and the subclaims' results |
| `numerical` | `{ "subject": { "closure_hash", "variant", "inherited_controls", "schedule_hash" }, "metric": "operator_norm", "precision_bits": n, "bound": decimal }` | **checker-computed, for every invocation context of the subject** (below): build the realised unitary U of that invocation (the region's realisation under the root schedule at that path, with its resolved classical arguments, budget, epsilon shares and cleanup choices, and `inherited_controls` control wires prepended) and the reference unitary V (the region's §5.3 semantics with the same arguments, controlled on the same wires) at the subject's signature widths in interval arithmetic at `precision_bits` (basis order = allocation order, controls first), form interval enclosures of `V†U − I` entrywise, derive the operator-norm bound as the Frobenius-norm upper bound in interval arithmetic, and verify it is ≤ `bound`; the claim holds iff it holds for every context |
| `channel` | `{ "subject": as above, "metric": "diamond", "precision_bits", "bound", "dual": [ { "Y": interval Hermitian matrix, "mu": decimal } … ] }` | for every invocation context of the subject, in enumeration order, verify its dual witness for the **diamond-norm SDP** below in interval arithmetic and `mu` ≤ `bound` (one witness per context; too few witnesses: reject) |

**CLAIM-REPLAY** (K13; b-isa-spec-9uy, from bennett-vm-clw).
Sampled observations remain evidence under the following replay contract.

**Comparison relation.** `exhaustive` and `sampled` claims
MUST name their `subject: { closure_hash, variant, inherited_controls,
schedule_hash? }` and an explicit `expected`; there is no implicit
self-comparison or default expected relation. Without a schedule the
left side is the subject's reference semantics at the bound invocation;
with one it is that invocation's realised operation. `expected: "oracle"`
compares the realised operation to §5.2's reference oracle map and
requires `schedule_hash`. [deps: ISA-5.2, ISA-11, ISA-12.1-P11] `expected: {program: H}` independently runs
the reference semantics of linked closure H at the **same** arguments,
budget and interface tuple; its signature MUST be compatible.
`expected: {table: rows}` compares to the explicit full-tuple rows
below. The checker MUST compare every interface output, preserve inputs
and borrowed systems, and verify clean workspace and exact +1 basis
phase for a permutation assertion (T3); matching a target or fault
projection alone, or a producer's claimed verdict, is insufficient. [deps: ISA-14.3-P3, ISA-5.6]
For a non-permutation realisation, use the existing numerical/channel
claims rather than labeling basis observations a permutation proof. [deps: ISA-12.1-P4, ISA-12.1-P5, ISA-14.2]
K4's `expected: "inverse_pair"` retains its separate two-composition
relation and entry/domain bindings; it is not a sampled proof option.

**Table tuples.** Each table row is `{ before: tuple,
after: tuple }`. A tuple is `{ bundles: ["<unsigned integer>", …],
borrowed: ["<unsigned integer>", …], regions: [["<cell>", …], …] }`.
`bundles` lists inherited control wires first (each width 1), then all
signature bundles in operand order, **including arbitrary initial
oracle target and fault bundles**. `borrowed` lists dirty bundles in
allocation order, with widths fixed by the checked borrow lifetimes;
`regions` lists interface regions in signature order, every cell in
increasing address order, each as an unsigned W-bit integer. All
integers use canonical decimal strings, within the declared widths;
no cells, inputs or unchanged components may be omitted. Workspace is
not an interface component and MUST finish zero. Rows are in domain
enumeration order, one per distinct declared domain element (including
for `sampled`); duplicate/missing/out-of-range rows reject. This favors
an explicit table; `{program: H}` provides the compact relation when a
full table is large. Sampling draws with replacement using the existing
sampler; coverage counts distinct drawn elements, not draws. A mismatch
rejects; success is always `evidence` with that distinct covered fraction
of D, including when the draws happen to cover all of D.

**CLAIM-REPLAY witness**

```json
{
  "invocation":{"args":["2"],"budget":["2","0","0","0"]},
  "row":{
    "before":{"bundles":["1","2","3"],"borrowed":["1"],"regions":[["2","1"]]},
    "after":{"bundles":["1","1","3"],"borrowed":["1"],"regions":[["2","1"]]}
  },
  "wrong_row":{
    "before":{"bundles":["1","2","3"],"borrowed":["1"],"regions":[["2","1"]]},
    "after":{"bundles":["1","2","3"],"borrowed":["1"],"regions":[["2","1"]]}
  },
  "changed_arg_after":{"bundles":["1","0","3"],"borrowed":["1"],"regions":[["2","1"]]},
  "short_budget_after":{"bundles":["1","2","2"],"borrowed":["1"],"regions":[["2","1"]]},
  "sampler":{"algorithm":"xoshiro256**","seed":["1","0","0","0"],"count":4},
  "widths":{"bundles":[2,2,4],"borrowed":[1],"regions":[[8,8]]},
  "sample_count":4,"distinct_samples":1,"covered_fraction":"1/33554432","verdict":"evidence"
}
```

The subject is a two-row oracle body `r = lift.add x a;`
`lift.retval r`, widths x = target = a = 2 and fault = 4, with a preserved
one-bit dirty bundle and an unused two-cell, eight-bit `in` interface
region; no inherited controls. [deps: ISA-5.4, ISA-7.2, ISA-12.1-P8] A legal layout has ptr_width=2, H=2, A_tab=1, S_cells=0,
null_size=1, no globals, the interface mapped at base 1 with extent 2,
heap_base=3 and heap_limit_max=0; the token is (2,0,0,0), and the unused
mapping is retained even though the body has no load/store. [deps: ISA-7.1, ISA-7.2, ISA-5.4]
The **body** does not access memory;
the interface cells remain part of D. [deps: ISA-7.2, ISA-12.2] The reference payload is
(1 + 2) mod 4 = 3: target 2 XOR 3 = 1 and fault 3 XOR 0 = 3.
The singleton declared domain is precisely `row.before`, within the
full 25-bit D (2+2+4+1+16 bits). [deps: ISA-5.4, ISA-7.2, ISA-12.1-P8, ISA-12.2] Four samples of singleton components draw no generator
words and cover one of 33554432 full tuples. [deps: ISA-12.1-P8, ISA-12.1-P13, ISA-12.2] An identity implementation
would agree with itself but MUST fail against `row`; using `wrong_row`
also MUST fail. At argument 1 the target is 2 XOR 2 = 0; at B = 1 the
payload is zero and fault is 3 XOR 1 = 2. A certificate for argument 2,
B = 2 cannot be replayed as either of those invocations. The printed
rows illustrate the relation; bound closure/object hashes still have
to be supplied in an actual certificate, not invented for this witness.

**Invocation contexts.** A subject denotes the set of invocations of
the region `closure_hash`, with the given `variant` and `inherited_
controls`, in the root schedule `schedule_hash`: every invocation of
the region in the root realisation's complete invocation tree —
including invocations that no schedule entry names and defaults alone
cover (§11) — with the classical arguments, budget, `epsilon_share`s and
cleanup realisations resolved for it. An invocation's **context key** is
its concrete invocation chain (§11), outermost first, each element `{
"symbol", "point", "iteration", "instance", "call_index" }` with every
iteration index, fixed-binding key and forward semantic-call occurrence
resolved
and, for an element inside an oracle, `"step"` (1..B) and `"phase"`
(`"fwd"` or `"rev"`) added; contexts are enumerated in increasing
bytewise order of the JCS encodings of their keys (wildcard selectors
play no part). [deps: ISA-3.7-P5, ISA-3.7-P6, ISA-9.2-P1.S3, ISA-11, ISA-11.1-P9]
Every claim bound and every
`error` entry (§12.3) is a **common upper bound over all contexts** of
its key; the multiplicity composition of §12.3 is sound for that reason.

**Diamond-norm SDP** (Watrous, *Theory of Computing* 5 (2009), §4; the
Hermiticity-preserving simplification). Let Φ = Φ_real − Φ_ref be the
difference of the subject's realised channel and the reference channel
(both on the subject's interface, controls included), d_in = d_out = d
the dimension. The **Choi matrix** is J(Φ) = Σ_{i,j} |i⟩⟨j| ⊗ Φ(|i⟩⟨j|)
(input factor first, output factor second, computational basis in
allocation order, **unnormalised**). The witness is a Hermitian Y of
dimension d² and a decimal mu. The checker accepts iff, with every
matrix entry an interval and every test rigorous (an inconclusive test
rejects): Y − J(Φ) ⪰ 0, Y + J(Φ) ⪰ 0, mu·I − Tr_out(Y) ⪰ 0 (partial trace
over the output factor), and mu ≤ `bound`; then ‖Φ‖◇ ≤ mu (the dual of
Watrous's SDP with Y₀ = Y₁ = Y; this is the diamond norm itself, not
half of it). *(non-normative)* With this input-first Choi convention
Tr_out traces the second factor, Tr[(ρ ⊗ I)Y] = Tr[ρ Tr_out(Y)]; a
primal formulation that labels the input factor ρᵀ ranges over the same
set of density matrices, so the bound is unchanged. Encoding: `Y` is a row-major array of entries `[re_lo, re_
hi, im_lo, im_hi]` (decimal strings). **Positivity test.** The checker
proves each of the three matrices **positive definite** by an interval
Cholesky (or Hermitian LDL†) factorisation in which the interval lower
bound of every pivot is strictly positive, and rejects otherwise; no
other test is admitted (nonnegative leading principal minors do not
imply positive semidefiniteness: diag(0, −1)). Producers make witnesses
strictly feasible by replacing Y with Y + δI and mu with mu′ = mu + δ·(d
+ 1) for a small rational δ > 0 (any mu′ > mu + δ·d suffices): then Y ±
J(Φ) ⪰ δI ≻ 0 and, since Tr_out(Y + δI) = Tr_out(Y) + δ·d·I, mu′·I −
Tr_out(Y + δI) ⪰ δI ≻ 0; ‖Φ‖◇ ≤ mu′, which MUST be ≤ `bound`. That
recipe assumes an exactly feasible witness. For a numerically obtained
witness the producer first certifies lower bounds −η₊, −η₋, −η_s on the
smallest eigenvalues of Y − J(Φ), Y + J(Φ) and mu·I − Tr_out(Y), chooses
δ > max(η₊, η₋, η_s) and publishes Y′ = Y + δI and mu′ = mu + (d + 1)·δ:
then Y′ ± J(Φ) ⪰ (δ − η±)I ≻ 0 and mu′·I − Tr_out(Y′) ⪰ (δ − η_s)I ≻ 0.
These margins make the matrices positive definite; they do not make the
interval test conclusive: every pivot enclosure the checker computes at
`precision_bits` MUST still have a strictly positive lower endpoint,
else the checker rejects (no fixed allowance such as 2^−`precision_bits`
bounds the interval error, which depends on d and conditioning). **Interval
arithmetic** at `precision_bits`
means every operation rounds outward to `precision_bits`-bit binary
rationals; transcendental entries (`rx ry rz`) are enclosed by a Taylor
series with the Lagrange remainder bound (§17 item 8 fixes the term
count). A `numerical` or `channel` subject's `schedule_hash` is required:
it names the realisation the claim is about. K13 also requires it for
`expected: "oracle"`; standalone reference relations use the explicit
invocation binding instead.

**Sampling.** For each domain component with range size R = hi − lo + 1,
assemble m = ⌈⌈log₂ R⌉/64⌉ consecutive generator words (least-significant
word first) into an integer, reduce modulo 2^⌈log₂ R⌉, reject and redraw
if ≥ R, add `lo`; components are consumed in enumeration order.

**Invocation identity witnesses (K63).** For `qinplace @G @G tk x`
at action A's `entry:0`, the two call edges below are distinct even with
identical arguments, and each prefix is also a round-trippable schedule
path selecting that invocation's descendants. [deps: ISA-4.5-P5, ISA-11-P5, ISA-11.1-P9, ISA-12.1-P11]
For two `lift.call` sites forwarding root token positions 0 and 1 into
lproc f, whose `entry:0` contains one `lift.oracle_call`, the next two
keys distinguish the row instances at outer step 1, even if token values
happen to agree. [deps: ISA-3.7-P5, ISA-3.7-P6, ISA-9.2-P1.S3, ISA-12.1-P11]
The `instance`/`call_index` fields are present in both schedule path
prefixes and context keys; context-only step/phase fields below are
supplied separately as §11 selectors in the schedule form. [deps: ISA-11-P3, ISA-11-P5, ISA-12.1-P11]

```json
{
  "qinplace_context_keys": [
    [
      {
        "symbol": "aaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaa",
        "point": "entry:0",
        "iteration": [],
        "instance": [],
        "call_index": 0
      }
    ],
    [
      {
        "symbol": "aaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaa",
        "point": "entry:0",
        "iteration": [],
        "instance": [],
        "call_index": 1
      }
    ]
  ],
  "lproc_context_keys": [
    [
      {
        "symbol": "cccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccc",
        "point": "entry:0",
        "iteration": [],
        "instance": [
          0
        ],
        "call_index": 0,
        "step": 1,
        "phase": "fwd"
      }
    ],
    [
      {
        "symbol": "cccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccccc",
        "point": "entry:0",
        "iteration": [],
        "instance": [
          1
        ],
        "call_index": 0,
        "step": 1,
        "phase": "fwd"
      }
    ]
  ]
}
```

**Expected-program correspondence (K73).** Compatibility for
`expected: {program: H}` requires the same ordered interface roles,
bundle widths/classes, region shapes/classes/roles, classical parameter
types and ordered borrowed-bundle widths/classes as the subject; local
names may differ. [deps: ISA-5.1-P1, ISA-9.4-P2, ISA-12.1-P7.S4, ISA-12.1-P8]
The checker initialises and compares each interface bundle by (group,
slot), each region cell by (group, slot, index), each borrowed bundle by
its checked lifetime position and each prepended inherited control by
position, using the complete before/after tuples of K13, never matching
components between the two programs by name. [deps: ISA-5.1-P1, ISA-9.4-P2, ISA-12.1-P7.S4, ISA-12.1-P8]

*(non-normative)* For subject mut slots named t,u and expected mut slots
named u,t, both one bit, the first slot is compared with the first and
the second with the second; exchanging the expected local names cannot
exchange the values compared. [deps: ISA-9.4-P2, ISA-12.1-P7.S4, ISA-12.1-P8]

### 12.2 Domains

`"domain": { "bundles": [ { "name", "width", "lo", "hi" } ], "borrowed": [ same ], "regions": [ { "name", "cells": "all" | [ { "index", "lo", "hi" } ] } ], "args"?: [ same as bundles ] }`,
bounds inclusive, enumeration order as listed, last component fastest.
`args` (inverse-pair claims only) ranges over classical arguments other
than tokens (whose tuples the entry's `arg_tokens` fixes, §12.3), each
read as an unsigned integer of its width (`ptr`: ptr_width; `f64`: 64,
its bits).
A `proof` label requires the domain to equal D; otherwise the claim is
`evidence` with the covered fraction.
For a borrow-frame obligation, use D_F of §5.6; a region claim over D
does not by itself establish the frame claim. For example, with c
allocated at zero, the single frame `CX(c,b)` over {c=0} × {b=0,1}
is identity on b. The interleaved prefix `CX a c; CX x c; CX a c;
CX x c` clears c only by polynomial algebra; §5.6 instead admits both
c values at entry, so the same single-CX frame fails its obligation.

**Component resolution (K69/K70).** These component-domain rules apply
to table/program/oracle relations; K4 inverse-pair claims retain their
separate two-composition relation and quantified argument bindings.
[deps: ISA-12.1-P7.S8, ISA-12.1-P1.S2, ISA-12.3-P3]
For an oracle the interface bundle groups are `in`, `tgt`, `fault` and its interface regions have role `in`;
for an action the bundle and region roles are `mut`, `in`, with slots
numbered from zero within each declared group. [deps: ISA-5.1-P1, ISA-5.2-P4, ISA-5.3-P1, ISA-9.4-P2]
A `bundles` or `regions` entry's `name` MUST be the subject header's
local name of an interface bundle or region of that category, resolving
to (group, slot); unknown names reject. [deps: ISA-5.1-P1, ISA-9.4-P2, ISA-12.2-P1]
For a subject with c inherited controls, the first c `bundles` entries
instead denote those prepended one-bit controls by position, with
label-only names; all c entries are required, and a singleton range
expresses a fixed control value. [deps: ISA-12.1-P8.S3, ISA-12.1-P7.S1, ISA-12.2-P1]
A region's `cells: "all"` denotes every cell (group, slot, index) in
increasing index order with its full W-bit range, and an explicit cell
list denotes those same cell identities in listed order with the stated
ranges. [deps: ISA-3.3-P19, ISA-12.2-P1.S1]
Entry k of `borrowed` denotes checked borrow lifetime k (zero-based) in
K13's tuple allocation order, independently of any physical storage
reuse; its `name` is only a label, may coincide with any other name and
never denotes an interface component. [deps: ISA-4.3-P2, ISA-5.6-P1, ISA-12.1-P8.S4]
A borrowed position beyond the checked borrow requirement rejects as
ill-formed. [deps: ISA-4.3-P2, ISA-12.1-P8.S4]
A `borrowed` entry MUST NOT discharge an interface bundle or region-cell
coverage obligation, whatever its name. [deps: ISA-4.3-P2, ISA-5.6-P1, ISA-12.1-P8]

**Domain validity and omission (K71/K72).** After resolution, each
component MUST occur at most once, every supplied `width` MUST equal
that component's width, ranges MUST satisfy 0 ≤ lo ≤ hi < 2^width, and
cell indices MUST satisfy 0 ≤ index < 2^A; a violation rejects the
claim as ill-formed. [deps: ISA-3.3-P2.S2, ISA-3.3-P19, ISA-12.2-P1]
Every omitted component is held at zero on every replayed element,
including omitted borrowed positions after the listed entries; workspace
and local regions retain their separate zero/image initialisations.
[deps: ISA-5.2-P4, ISA-5.3-P1, ISA-12.2-P1]
The domain denotes the product of the resolved ranges with those
omitted components fixed at zero, not the full range of an omitted
component. [deps: ISA-5.2-P4, ISA-5.3-P1, ISA-12.2-P1]
For an exhaustive claim its element count n is the product of the range
sizes over individual resolved components, the empty product being 1,
and its covered fraction is exactly n/|D|, with |D| the product of the
full component range sizes (prepended inherited controls included).
[deps: ISA-5.2-P4, ISA-5.3-P1, ISA-12.1-P8, ISA-12.2-P1]

**Coverage and verdicts (K69/K74).** For exhaustive and sampled claims,
`proof` and `evidence` are checker-assigned outcomes recorded in the
checker report against the region closure hash and zero-based `claims`
array index, with the bound invocation or invocation context key where
applicable; they are not producer assertions in the certificate.
[deps: ISA-1.2-P4, ISA-12.1-P7, ISA-12.1-P11, ISA-12.3-P1, ISA-12.3-P3]
An exhaustive table/program/oracle claim receives `proof` iff every
obligation of §12.1 succeeds on every element and its domain equals D
component by
component: every interface bundle, every interface region cell, every
borrowed position and every prepended inherited control occurs exactly
once with its full range. [deps: ISA-12.1-P1, ISA-12.1-P7, ISA-12.1-P8, ISA-5.2-P4, ISA-5.3-P1, ISA-14.3-P3]
This equality is decided over resolved identities and ranges, never
sets of names; an explicit full-range list of every cell covers exactly
the same region domain as `cells: "all"`. [deps: ISA-3.3-P19, ISA-12.2-P1, ISA-12.1-P8]
Successful exhaustive checks on a strict slice receive `evidence` with
n replayed elements and n/|D|; successful sampled checks receive
`evidence` with exactly `count` replayed draws and K13's distinct-draw
fraction, even if it is 1. [deps: ISA-12.1-P1, ISA-12.1-P2, ISA-12.1-P8.S9, ISA-12.1-P8.S10, ISA-12.2-P1]
A supplied `label` of `proof` or `evidence` is only a request: the
checker MUST compute the outcome independently and reject a requested
`proof` when a completed check yields only `evidence`. [deps: ISA-1.2-P4, ISA-12.1-P7.S6, ISA-12.2-P1]
Neither an evidence label nor a requested label discharges an obligation
requiring proof over D. [deps: ISA-5.6-P2, ISA-14.3-P3, ISA-12.3]

**Host limits (K75).** Domain widths, region address width A, cell-list
lengths and sampler `count` have no ISA-imposed upper cap; a checker
MUST NOT treat its host's representability, allocation or replay limits
as domain illegality. [deps: ISA-3.3-P2.S2, ISA-3.3-P19, ISA-12.1-P2, ISA-12.2-P1]
If a checker cannot complete a legal claim on its host, including
materialising an element, enumerating the domain or retaining the
required replay/coverage data, it MUST terminate normally and record
**unchecked (host limit)** for that claim in the same report, citing
§12.2 and the quantity or operation that exceeded the host limit.
[deps: ISA-12.1-P1, ISA-12.1-P2, ISA-12.1-P8, ISA-12.2-P1]
That outcome is distinct from rejection and from either label, supplies
no evidence count or covered fraction for the claim, and discharges no
obligation of §5.6, T3 or §12.3, including when a producer requested a
label. [deps: ISA-1.2-P4, ISA-5.6-P2, ISA-14.3-P3, ISA-12.3]

**COMPONENT-COVERAGE witness**
The following full tuples and resolved identities instantiate the
component rules without a schedule or inherited controls.
[deps: ISA-4.4, ISA-12.1-P8, ISA-12.2-P1]

```json
{
  "mut_names":["t","u"],"widths":[1,1],"borrowed_widths":[1],
  "domain":{"bundles":[{"name":"u","width":1,"lo":0,"hi":1}],"borrowed":[{"name":"t","width":1,"lo":0,"hi":1}],"regions":[]},
  "resolved":[["mut",1],["borrowed",0]],
  "elements":4,"full_elements":8,"covered_fraction":"1/2","verdict":"evidence",
  "renamed_borrowed":"b",
  "counterexample":{
    "before":{"bundles":["1","0"],"borrowed":["0"],"regions":[]},
    "cnot_after":{"bundles":["1","1"],"borrowed":["0"],"regions":[]},
    "identity_after":{"bundles":["1","0"],"borrowed":["0"],"regions":[]}
  },
  "full_bundle_order":["t","u"],"first_mismatch_index":4,
  "region":{"A":1,"W":1,"cells":[{"index":1,"lo":0,"hi":1},{"index":0,"lo":0,"hi":1}],"elements":4,"covers_all":true}
}
```

*(non-normative)* For the one-bit mut t,u action that borrows b,
applies `cx t u` and returns b, compared with identity with the same
borrow requirement, the four elements above agree because omitted t
is zero, while (t,u,b)=(1,0,0) distinguishes the programs; renaming the
borrowed label cannot change the domain or its verdict. [deps: ISA-4.4, ISA-4.3-P2, ISA-12.1-P7, ISA-12.2-P1]
Adding the full t range in the printed full-bundle order exposes the
mismatch at zero-based element 4, rather than yielding proof.
[deps: ISA-4.4, ISA-12.1-P1, ISA-12.2-P1]
The two-cell region's explicit reversed cell list covers all four basis
configurations, while repeating index 1 or using index 2 is ill-formed;
an action region[63,1] with `cells: "all"` and `count: 1` remains legal but has
2^63 bits per element, so an unable host reports unchecked (host limit).
[deps: ISA-3.3-P19, ISA-12.1-P2, ISA-12.2-P1]

### 12.3 Schema and composition

```
  versions; bindings { object_hash, interface_hash, closure_hash (required), reference_program_hash, dialect_mapping_version, target_hash, schedule_hash?, invocation? (K13) }
  producer { name, version, materialisation { llvm_version, pipeline, normaliser_version } }
  regions { <closure_hash>: { kind, signature, bounds, estimate? (K12), frame (required typed-record descriptor, §9.4), resources { name: decimal }, gates_used { gate: { count, scheme?, epsilon? } },
            claims: [ <claim> … ], verification: { oracle: i, borrow_frames: [i], inverse_pairs: [ { g, ginv, claim: i, budget: [B,heap,stack,depth], arg_tokens?: { "<arg>": [B,heap,stack,depth] } } ], unitary: [i], channel: [i],
                                                   fault_rate: { domain, faults_by_code, undefined_excluded, layout_dependent_excluded } },
            cleanup: { "<variant>:<inherited_controls>": [ { path, realisation } ] },
            error:   { "<variant>:<inherited_controls>": { local: { "operator_norm"?: v, "diamond"?: v }, inclusive: { "operator_norm"?: v, "diamond"?: v } } },
            definedness_assumptions: [ids] } }
  composition { multiplicities: { "<hash>:<variant>:<inherited_controls>": n (from the realised schedule) }, epsilon_total: { "diamond": v }, method: "sum_over_multiplicities" }
```

**Invocation binding (K13).** An invocation is
`{ args: [value, …], budget: [B,heap,stack,depth] }`, with every value
resolved before replay and all four budget components explicit even
when zero. `args` is in signature order: `iN` and `ptr` values are
canonical **unsigned decimal strings** of exactly that bit-vector
value (ptr has `ptr_width` bits); `f64` is `0x` followed by exactly 16
lowercase hex digits of its IEEE 754 binary64 bit pattern, most
significant nibble first (so −0 and NaN payloads are not lost); a token
is its four-component tuple of canonical nonnegative decimal strings,
not a process-local token id. Budget components are also canonical
nonnegative decimal strings. Signature types disambiguate encodings;
widths/ranges and every tuple MUST pass §5.4 validation. `params_hash`
is SHA-256 of the UTF-8 JCS **args array**, no trailing LF (§9.2), not
of the whole invocation. Thus equal token tuples have equal parameter
hashes regardless of ids, while +0 and −0 `f64` parameters differ.

Each standalone table/program/structural claim MUST carry its own
`invocation`; it binds
**every** classical argument and budget. Missing values have no implicit
zero/default. Claims with no arguments still bind `args: []` and their
budget. K4 inverse-pair claims instead bind the entry's budget,
`arg_tokens` and quantified `args` domain as already specified; this
rule does not replace that quantification by one chosen argument.
A parameter-uniform structural claim MAY instead bind
`invocation: { args: "all", budget: "all" }`: its checker MUST discharge
the rule's side conditions for every well-typed argument vector and
valid budget, not by replaying a chosen instance. Thus generic library
and adjoint/translation proofs retain their universal scope.
A scheduled certificate MUST carry the root `bindings.invocation`;
its args hash and budget MUST equal the root schedule's bindings, and
all nested arguments and budgets MUST be resolved at each realised
invocation context (§12.1), including default-covered contexts.
Explicit nested bindings, if supplied, MUST equal those resolutions;
a root binding MUST NOT be silently reused for a different callee.
A checker MUST reject absent or conflicting bindings before replay,
and an executor may use a verified result only for the invocation(s)
it actually covers. Reference table/program/structural claims need no
schedule; they prove only their reference relation, not a target
realisation. Scheduled/numerical/channel claims retain the expansion
library gate and all existing context and composition obligations.

**CLAIM-BINDING witness**

```json
{"signature":["i128","f64","token"],"args":["18446744073709551617","0x8000000000000000",["2","0","0","0"]],"params_hash":"9f628a694fa7259bf899bfb677aabce1c084e268a738a40f7f643eb6d8c9c093"}
```

The first argument is > 2^64; the second is binary64 negative zero;
the third is a token tuple, not an id. Replacing negative zero with
`0x0000000000000000` changes `params_hash`; replacing the token by
another valid id of the same tuple does not.

`versions` is the object `{ "isa", "object", "launch", "sched", "cert",
"coverage", "target" }` of the seven header versions. A **variant** is a
nonempty string chosen by the producer (the ISA enumerates none); it is
compared bytewise.

`inclusive(R, key) = local(R, key) + Σ_callees n_c × inclusive(c, key_c)`
**per metric**, keyed like `cleanup` by `<variant>:<inherited_controls>`
(the realised error depends on both); every `local` value is a verified
common upper bound over all invocation contexts of its key in the bound
root schedule (§12.1), so multiplying by n_c is sound; an
`operator_norm` value converts
to `diamond` by ×2 once, at the point it is first combined with a
`diamond` value; `operator_norm` conclusions (T6, controlled unitary
variants) require `operator_norm` bounds throughout. A controlled
unitary variant requires a `numerical` claim; an ε > 0 realisation
compared as a channel requires a `channel` claim. Executable claims
(`numerical`, `channel`, and `exhaustive` or `sampled` with
`expected: "oracle"`, plus table/program comparisons of a realised
operation) bind to `schedule_hash` through their subject. Standalone
reference relations have only the invocation scope defined above (K13).

Checker diagnostic codes other than codes explicitly mandated elsewhere
in this specification are implementation-defined. A checker SHOULD
document its stable code meanings and report the failed normative
obligation and certificate location with a rejection. Such codes are
scoped by checker name and version; their spelling does not alter the
verdict or any executor status/fault code. This convention does not
replace mandated E_SCHEDULE_INFEASIBLE behavior or K55 diagnostics.
